It doesn't require CUB and works for any block size. We can implement block reduce with warp shuffle and a small shared memory array for warp sums. Implementation of block reduce with warp shuffle: ``` __inline__ __device__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { for (int offset = warpSize/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); return val; } __inline__ __device__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { static __shared__ float shared[32]; // max warps per block int lane = threadIdx.x % warpSize; int wid = threadIdx.x / warpSize; val = warp_reduce_sum(val); if (lane == 0) shared[wid] = val; __syncthreads(); // read from shared memory only if that warp existed val = (threadIdx.x < blockDim.x) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; if (wid == 0) val = warp_reduce_sum(val); return val; } ``` This is standard and avoids CUB entirely. It's also easy and robust. Since H100 has warp size 32, this is fine. With this, we don't need CUB, but we need to include nothing extra. However, the existing code uses CUB. Vector kernel design: ```cpp template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_vec_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; if (row >= num_tokens) return; const int tid = threadIdx.x; using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>; const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE; const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size); const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight); VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size); float variance = 0.0f; // Load and accumulate #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = input_vec[idx]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); variance += v * v; } // We need to store val somewhere for the second phase. Since we don't know if idx is unique per thread across the loop, // we can't easily keep it in registers for multiple iterations. However, for the common case where BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size, // the loop runs at most once. We can optimize for that. } // Block reduce // Warp shuffle // ... ``` To handle the general case, we could store the loaded vectors in shared memory to avoid re-loading. Let's allocate shared memory for the row. But shared memory complicates the kernel because we need to know the size at compile time or use dynamic shared memory. We know hidden_size at runtime. Dynamic shared memory is easy: `extern __shared__ char smem[];` but we need to pass size at launch: `kernel<<<grid, block, shared_mem_size, stream>>>()`. The shared memory size per block is passed as the third argument. So we can do: ```cpp VecT* shmem = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(smem); for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = input_vec[idx]; shmem[idx] = val; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); variance += v * v; } } __syncthreads(); ``` Then after reduction,: ```cpp for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = shmem[idx]; VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; ... out_vec[idx] = result; } ``` But note: the `idx` used in the second loop must correspond to the same position as in the first loop. Since both loops iterate with same start, step, they will map the same thread to the same idx only if `tid` maps to the same `idx`. Actually `idx = tid + k*BLOCK_SIZE`. So in both loops, the thread processes the same set of indices. Thus `shmem[idx]` is accessible. However, if multiple threads write to the same `shmem[idx]`, that's a race. But each `idx` is processed by exactly one thread in the first loop because `idx` is unique across the block for a given iteration. For iteration k, thread tid writes to `shmem[tid + k*BLOCK_SIZE]`. In the second loop, the same thread reads `shmem[tid + k*BLOCK_SIZE]`. So no race. Good. But we need to ensure `shmem` is sized to `vec_hidden_size * sizeof(VecT)`. We pass that at launch. This single-pass with shmem works for any block size. Shared memory usage: for hidden_size=4096, fp16, VEC_SIZE=8 => 512 * 16 = 8192 bytes. Plus maybe 128 bytes for warp reduce. Total ~8.5KB. If hidden_size=65536 and block size 1024, each SM can run at most 1 block due to shmem limit (128KB < 227KB) but maybe 1 block per SM. That's fine. The first read brings it to L2. The second read hits L2. So global re-read may be almost as fast as shmem. In fact, for data that fits in L2, the second read is cheap. Now, let's write the kernel carefully. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cub/cub.cuh> #define VEC_SIZE 8 template <typename T, int N> struct alignas(sizeof(T) * N) Vec { T data[N]; }; // Block reduce using warp shuffle template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { // Warp reduce #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize / 2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } __shared__ float shared[BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize]; int lane = threadIdx.x % warpSize; int wid = threadIdx.x / warpSize; if (lane == 0) { shared[wid] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Read from shared memory val = (threadIdx.x < blockDim.x) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; if (wid == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize / 2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } } return val; } template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_vec_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; if (row >= num_tokens) return; const int tid = threadIdx.x; using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>; const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE; const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size); const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight); VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size); float variance = 0.0f; // Phase 1: load and accumulate #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = input_vec[idx]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); variance += v * v; } } // Block reduce variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); // Phase 2: compute and write #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = input_vec[idx]; VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[i]); result.data[i] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx] = result; } // Tail: handle remaining elements if hidden_size % VEC_SIZE != 0 int tail_start = vec_hidden_size * VEC_SIZE; for (int idx = tail_start + tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]); out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } } ``` Wait, in the second vector loop, we reload from global memory. To avoid that, we could store in shared memory. But as discussed, reloading from L2 is okay. But if we want to be truly single-pass for DRAM, we need shmem. Given the problem says we have 30 tool calls and limited turns, we should try a version, compile, test, iterate. The simplest robust version is the two-pass vector kernel with block size 512. That should already be faster than the original because original used block size 256 for num_tokens=512. Let's estimate: original block size 256 gave 65% occupancy. Our block size 512 gives 100% occupancy (with queued blocks). Also we use inline warp shuffle instead of CUB (maybe faster). And we use compile-time BLOCK_SIZE. The vector loads are the same. But can we do better by using shared memory to avoid second read? Let's think about memory bandwidth. For 512 tokens * 4096 hidden * 2 bytes = 4MB per tensor. Original: read input (4MB), read weight (8KB), read input again (4MB), write output (4MB). Total ~12MB. Our two-pass: read input (4MB), read weight (8KB), read input again (4MB), write output (4MB). Total ~12MB. Wait, our kernel also reads input twice: once in phase 1 and once in phase 2. So DRAM traffic is same as original if L2 doesn't retain it. However, with block size 512, the second read likely hits L2 because the first read was just moments ago and L2 is 50MB, can hold all rows? Actually 512 * 4096 * 2 = 4MB. Yes, the entire input fits in L2. So the second read hits L2, reducing effective DRAM traffic to ~8MB. The original kernel with block size 256 and 512 blocks also had the same property: first read brings to L2, second read hits L2. So both have effective ~8MB DRAM traffic. But our kernel may be faster due to better occupancy. To further improve, we can use shmem to guarantee single pass. But is the complexity worth it? Maybe. Let's also consider using TMA (Tensor Memory Accelerator) on Hopper. TMA allows async global-to-shared transfers. But it's complex to use with inline extension and not necessary for this simple kernel. Another optimization: Use `__launch_bounds__(512, 3)` to tell compiler max threads and min blocks per SM to control register usage. For block size 512, to have 3 blocks per SM, total registers per SM = 64K. So per thread max = 65536*32 bits / (512*3) = 1365 32-bit registers? Wait, register file is 64K 32-bit registers per SM. So for 1536 threads, max registers per thread = 65536 / 1536 = 42.6. That's 42 registers per thread. But the kernel uses very few registers, so we are fine. We don't need to force register limit. Wait, 64K 32-bit registers per SM. Max registers per thread is 255. But if we have 512 threads and want 3 blocks, total threads = 1536. 65536 / 1536 = 42 registers per thread. If the kernel uses more than 42 registers, occupancy drops below 3 blocks. The compiler might use more if not constrained. But our kernel is simple: a few floats, vector struct. It likely uses less than 32 registers. So 3 blocks is achievable. We can add `__launch_bounds__(512)` to help compiler. Let's count registers: - `val`: 8 halfs = 8 registers? Or compiler optimizes. - `w`: 8 halfs = 8 registers. - `result`: 8 halfs = 8 registers. - `variance`, `v`, `wf`, indices, pointers. Maybe ~30 registers. It should fit. We can also use `#pragma unroll` for loops. Since the loop count for block size 512 and vec_hidden_size=512 is exactly 1, the loop unrolls automatically. For general case, unroll 4 is fine. Let's also consider using `__restrict__` on pointers to allow compiler optimizations. One more thing: `block_reduce_sum` uses `shared[BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize]`. For BLOCK_SIZE=512, shared size = 16 floats = 64 bytes. This is small. Now, about scalar tail: If `hidden_size % VEC_SIZE != 0`, the tail elements need to be processed. But the tail loop in the code above has a race? No, each thread handles tail elements with stride BLOCK_SIZE, and each index is unique. The `out` write is fine. However, the tail loop writes to `out` but does not handle `residual` or `input` beyond vectorized part. That's fine. But the tail loop reads from `input` again. That's okay. One subtle bug: In the tail, I used `input[row * hidden_size + idx]`. But `input` is a 2D tensor. If the input is flattened, `row * hidden_size` is correct. In the launcher, we flatten. Also, `weight` is 1D, so `weight[idx]` is fine. Now, we need to ensure alignment for vector loads. We assume pointer is aligned to `sizeof(VecT)`. For PyTorch CUDA tensors, the base pointer is aligned to at least 16 bytes (sometimes 256). For row start `input + row * hidden_size`, since `hidden_size * sizeof(scalar_t)` is a multiple of 16 (4096*2=8192), each row is aligned. So pointer is aligned. We should also verify `weight` pointer alignment. It is contiguous and base aligned. We might want to add a check and fallback if not aligned. But for evaluation, it's aligned. To be safe, we can add a scalar fallback launcher that is called if alignment is not met. But let's keep it simple: always call vector kernel if hidden_size % VEC_SIZE == 0, else call scalar kernel. For alignment, we can cast `uintptr_t` and check. Let's write the scalar kernel too. ```cpp template <typename scalar_t, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_scalar_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; if (row >= num_tokens) return; const int tid = threadIdx.x; float variance = 0.0f; #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); variance += v * v; } variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]); out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } } ``` This is also two-pass but simple. Now, the host launcher: ```cpp torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps) { TORCH_CHECK(input.is_cuda()); TORCH_CHECK(weight.is_cuda()); TORCH_CHECK(input.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half); TORCH_CHECK(weight.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half); auto input_2d = input.reshape({-1, input.size(-1)}); int num_tokens = input_2d.size(0); int hidden_size = input_2d.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(input_2d); const int VEC_SIZE = 8; int block_size = 512; // Ensure block_size does not exceed hidden_size if (block_size > hidden_size) block_size = hidden_size; block_size = (block_size + 31) / 32 * 32; // round up to warp multiple if (block_size == 0) block_size = 32; dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(block_size); // We need to dispatch block size at runtime. However, given we only need to handle hidden_size=4096, we can heavily simplify: just use block_size=512 and vector kernel. No need for extensive dispatch. The contract says HIDDEN_SIZE=4096 and NUM_TOKENS=512. It might test with other sizes? Usually the evaluation uses those constants exactly, but could also test with slight variations. Let's search memory: In CUB 1.8, `BlockReduce` does have: ``` /// \brief Computes a threadblock-wide reduction for thread<sub>0</sub>, ..., thread<sub><tt>blockDim.x</tt>-1</sub> using the specified binary reduction functor. The first \p num_valid threads are reduced. /// /// \tparam ReduceOp Binary reduction operator type having member <tt>T operator()(const T &a, const T &b)</tt> /// \param[in] input Calling thread's input /// \param[in] reduction_op Binary reduction operator /// \param[in] num_valid Number of valid threads to reduce __device__ __forceinline__ T Reduce( T input, ReduceOp reduction_op, int num_valid); ``` I think this exists. But I'm not 100% sure. Another safe approach: use CUB with `BLOCK_SIZE=1024` and use `Reduce` (without num_valid) but ensure all threads participate. Threads with no work can pass 0.0f. That works! Because the reduction is sum, and extra threads contribute 0. So we can allocate block_size up to 1024, and all threads call the reduce even if they have no data. This is safe and standard. So we can use: ``` using BlockReduce = cub::BlockReduce<float, 1024>; __shared__ typename BlockReduce::TempStorage temp_storage; float variance = 0.0f; // loop ... variance = BlockReduce(temp_storage).Reduce(variance, cub::Sum()); ``` This works regardless of actual block size, as long as blockDim.x <= 1024. All threads must reach the reduce call. So if we have block_size=512, all 512 threads participate, and the extra 512 (non-existent) don't matter because CUB uses `blockDim.x`? Actually, `cub::BlockReduce<float, 1024>` is designed for a block of up to 1024 threads. The actual number of threads is `blockDim.x`. CUB uses `blockDim.x` internally. Wait, no: CUB algorithms often use the template parameter `BLOCK_DIM_X` and assume the block has exactly that many threads. If the actual block has fewer threads, behavior may be undefined because some shared memory may be uninitialized or some threads won't participate in barriers. Actually, CUB `BlockReduce` does not assume all template-parameter threads exist; it uses `blockDim.x`. Let's check: CUB's internal `BlockReduce` uses `BLOCK_THREADS = BLOCK_DIM_X` to size temp storage. It also uses `blockDim.x` for loops? Actually I recall that CUB algorithms require all threads in the block to participate, and the template parameter should match `blockDim.x`. Or at least `blockDim.x` should be equal to the template parameter. If not, using a larger template parameter may be okay if all threads still call into it, but the internal barriers synchronize all `blockDim.x` threads. Since the temp storage is sized for `BLOCK_DIM_X`, it's larger than needed but that's fine. However, some CUB algorithms may use thread indices up to `BLOCK_DIM_X`. If `blockDim.x` < `BLOCK_DIM_X`, threads with `threadIdx.x >= blockDim.x` don't exist, so they won't participate. The algorithm may break if it expects those threads to participate in `__syncthreads()`. But CUB's block algorithms typically only synchronize within `blockDim.x`? Actually `__syncthreads()` synchronizes all threads in the block. CUB might not call `__syncthreads()` if not needed, but some internal steps do. To be safe, we can use `cub::BlockReduce<float, 1024>` and assume `blockDim.x <= 1024`. Many codebases do this. Or we can just use warp shuffle and avoid CUB altogether. Let's draft the code: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cuda_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> template <typename T, int N> struct alignas(sizeof(T) * N) Vec { T data[N]; variance += v * v; } variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]); out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } } // Host launcher torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps) { TORCH_CHECK(input.is_cuda(), "input must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(weight.is_cuda(), "weight must be CUDA"); int num_tokens = input_2d.size(0); int hidden_size = input_2d.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(input_2d); const int VEC_SIZE = 8; int block_size = 512; if (block_size > hidden_size) block_size = hidden_size; The `cpp_sources` should have the declaration, and `cuda_sources` should include the declaration and definition. In `load_inline`, the `cpp_sources` are compiled as C++ and `cuda_sources` are compiled as CUDA. The function must be declared in the cpp source for pybind to see it. Actually, `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` handles binding automatically if you pass `functions` list. It generates pybind wrappers for functions listed in `functions`. The `cpp_sources` and `cuda_sources` are concatenated? Let's check typical usage. Typical usage of `load_inline`: ```python module = load_inline( name="...", cpp_sources="...", cuda_sources="...", functions=["rms_norm_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3"], verbose=True, ) ``` The `cpp_sources` usually contains the C++ function declarations (maybe also definitions). The `cuda_sources` contains the CUDA code. The extension builder compiles them together. Sometimes it's easier to put everything in `cuda_sources` and leave `cpp_sources` empty. But you need the function to be declared `extern "C"` or just as a regular C++ function for pybind. `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` generates a pybind11 wrapper for each function in `functions`. It looks for the function declaration in the combined sources. So as long as the function is declared somewhere (e.g., in `cpp_sources` or `cuda_sources`), it's fine. I usually put both declaration and definition in `cuda_sources`, and put the declaration in `cpp_sources` to ensure the C++ compiler sees it before the pybind wrapper. But if `cpp_sources` only has the declaration, and `cuda_sources` has the definition, it should link. Alternatively, we can put everything in `cuda_sources` and set `cpp_sources=''`. The pybind wrapper is generated after all sources. But to be safe, I'll include the declaration in `cpp_sources` and the definition in `cuda_sources`. Actually, the CUDA source will also include the declaration implicitly if we define it there. But the C++ compiler might complain about undefined reference if it doesn't see it. Wait, `cpp_sources` are compiled by host C++ compiler. `cuda_sources` are compiled by nvcc. The pybind wrapper is in a separate generated file that includes the declarations. It needs to see the function declarations. So we must include the declaration in `cpp_sources`. Let's do: `cpp_sources = "torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps);"` In `cuda_sources`, we include `#include <torch/extension.h>` and the definition. One more thing: The `block_reduce_sum` uses `__shared__ float shared[BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize];`. If BLOCK_SIZE is 32, shared size is 1. If BLOCK_SIZE is 512, size is 16. That's fine. But in the device function `block_reduce_sum`, the `__shared__` array is declared inside the function. This is allowed; it'll be placed in the same shared memory. However, because it's templated, each instantiation will allocate its own static shared memory? Actually, `__shared__` inside a templated function is okay; the size depends on template parameter. For a given kernel, there's only one instantiation, so it's fine. Wait, but `block_reduce_sum` is called twice in the kernel (once for variance, but only once actually). It's fine. Another issue: The kernel uses `__launch_bounds__(BLOCK_SIZE, 3)` to help compiler allocate registers. For block size 512, max blocks per SM is 3 (1536/512). This is fine. For block size 256, max blocks per SM is 6, but we specify 3, which may limit register usage more aggressively, potentially spilling. Actually, `__launch_bounds__(max_threads_per_block, min_blocks_per_multiprocessor)` tells the compiler that the kernel will be launched with at most `max_threads_per_block` threads per block, and at least `min_blocks_per_multiprocessor` blocks per SM. This helps the compiler decide register allocation to ensure at least `min_blocks_per_multiprocessor` blocks can fit. Setting `min_blocks_per_multiprocessor=3` is a moderate target. The second `__shfl_down_sync` across warp 0 will sum the values loaded by each lane. But `shared[lane]` will read uninitialized shared memory for lanes >= number of warps. For example, block size = 64 => 2 warps. `shared` has size `BLOCK_SIZE/warpSize = 2`. After the first warp reduce, warp 0 writes `shared[0]` and warp 1 writes `shared[1]`. Then all threads read `shared[lane]`. For lane=0..31, lane 0 reads shared[0], lane 1 reads shared[1], lanes 2..31 read out of bounds! That's a bug. We need to fix the block reduce. Correct block reduce with warp shuffle: ``` template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { int lane = threadIdx.x % 32; int wid = threadIdx.x / 32; // warp reduce #pragma unroll for (int mask = 16; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } if (lane == 0) { shared[wid] = val; } __syncthreads(); val = (lane < (BLOCK_SIZE / 32)) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; if (wid == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = 16; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } } return val; } ``` Wait, if block size is 32, wid is either 0 or 1? Actually for block size 32, wid = 0 for all threads (since threadIdx.x / 32 = 0). `BLOCK_SIZE/32 = 1`. `lane < 1` means only lane 0 reads `shared[0]`. Then in `wid == 0`, all threads in warp 0 participate in shfl. But only lane 0 has the correct val; other lanes have 0. The reduce across warp 0 will sum them: lane 0's val (the total) plus zeros from others. The final result is in lane 0 (and all lanes after shfl?). Actually `__shfl_down_sync` broadcasts within warp but the final val is only in lane 0? Wait, after the loop, lane 0 has the sum. But we don't need it in all threads; only thread 0 will write to `s_variance`. However, we do `if (tid == 0) s_variance = ...`. So it's okay if only lane 0 has the final sum. But to be safe, we can broadcast the result: ``` if (wid == 0) { #pragma unroll ... val += __shfl_down_sync(...); } __syncthreads(); if (threadIdx.x == 0) shared[0] = val; __syncthreads(); return shared[0]; ``` But that adds overhead. Simpler: After warp 0 reduces, broadcast to all threads via shared memory: ``` if (wid == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize/2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } } if (threadIdx.x == 0) { shared[0] = val; } __syncthreads(); return shared[0]; ``` Wait, `val` in warp 0 after the loop contains the total sum in lane 0. The memory is shared across the block. But if multiple threads call the function, it's the same memory. So `__shared__ float shared;` works. Let's simplify block reduce: ```cpp template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { // Warp reduce #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize / 2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } __shared__ float shared; int lane = threadIdx.x % warpSize; if (lane == 0) { shared = val; } __syncthreads(); // Only first warp reads the partial sums if (threadIdx.x < warpSize) { val = shared; } else { val = 0.0f; } // Wait, we need each warp's sum to be in a separate slot. With a single shared float, // only one warp sum can be stored. That's wrong. ``` Ah right. We need an array of size `BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize`. So: ```cpp template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize / 2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } __shared__ float shared[BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize]; int lane = threadIdx.x % warpSize; int wid = threadIdx.x / warpSize; if (lane == 0) { shared[wid] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Load the partial sums into warp 0 if (wid == 0) { val = (lane < BLOCK_SIZE / warpSize) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int mask = warpSize / 2; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } } // Broadcast to all threads if (threadIdx.x == 0) { shared[0] = val; } __syncthreads(); return shared[0]; } ``` This is safe. For block size 32, `BLOCK_SIZE/warpSize = 1`. Warp 0 does the second reduction: `val = (lane < 1) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f;` => lane 0 gets `shared[0]`, others 0. Warp 0: lane 0 reads shared[0], lane 1 reads shared[1], others 0. Warp reduce sums lane0+lane1+0s = total. Thread 0 writes to shared[0]. All return shared[0]. Good. This works. } out_vec[idx] = result; } int tail_start = vec_hidden_size * VEC_SIZE; for (int idx = tail_start + tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); } } torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps) { TORCH_CHECK(input.is_cuda(), "input must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(weight.is_cuda(), "weight must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(input.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half, "Only fp16 supported"); Each thread loads 16 bytes, does 8 half-to-float conversions, 8 FMAs (actually v*v into acc, then later v*s*w). Wait, in phase 1, each thread does 8 conversions and 8 FMAs (multiply-add to variance). In phase 2, each thread loads input again (16 bytes), weight (16 bytes), does 8 conversions, 8 multiplies, 8 stores. So per thread, ~16 loads, 8 stores, ~24 arithmetic ops. The arithmetic intensity is tiny. Memory bound. DRAM traffic: input read 4MB, weight read 8KB, input read again 4MB (from L2 likely), output write 4MB. Total ~12MB from L2 perspective, 8MB from DRAM. If second read hits L2, bandwidth is huge (~10-20 TB/s). So saving it doesn't dramatically change runtime. The main bottleneck is the first read from DRAM and the write. So eliminating second read may not be a huge win. But if we could store the vectors in shared memory, we avoid L2 read and reduce latency. However, writing to shmem and reading back also takes cycles. Given the time constraints, the current design is solid and should be a significant improvement over the original due to better occupancy, block size calibration, and warp shuffle reduction. One more optimization: In the second loop, we reload `input_vec[idx]`. We could instead store it in registers between loops, but we can't because of `__syncthreads()`. Actually we can! The compiler doesn't require variables to be volatile across `__syncthreads()`. We can declare `VecT local_val;` before the sync, assign it in the first loop, then use it after the sync. But the first loop may run multiple iterations (if block_size < vec_hidden_size). In our case, block_size >= vec_hidden_size (we set block_size = vec_size up to 512). So the loop runs at most once. So we can store the vector in a register. Specifically: ```cpp VecT local_val; bool has_val = false; #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { local_val = input_vec[idx]; has_val = true; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_val.data[i]); variance += v * v; } } variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); if (has_val) { VecT w = weight_vec[tid]; // use tid because idx = tid when loop runs once ... out_vec[tid] = result; } ``` But this only works if each thread processes exactly one vector (i.e., `BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size`). If `BLOCK_SIZE < vec_hidden_size`, threads loop multiple times and we can't keep all in registers. To keep it generic, we can add an `if constexpr` or template specialization for the case `BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size`? But `vec_hidden_size` is runtime. We can simply have the generic loop. The compiler may optimize it well. For the case where the loop runs once, it will keep `val` in registers. Actually, in the first loop, the compiler sees that `idx` is only `tid` because the loop condition `idx < vec_hidden_size` and `idx += BLOCK_SIZE` with `BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size` means it runs once. So it will unroll and keep `val` in registers. But after `__syncthreads()`, the compiler may still have `val` in registers if not overwritten. However, we re-declare `VecT val` in the second loop, so the compiler will reload. If we reuse the same `val` variable, we might avoid reload. But we assigned `val` inside the loop scope. To reuse it, we need to declare it outside. Let's try: ```cpp VecT val_reg; bool has_data = false; #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { val_reg = input_vec[idx]; has_data = true; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val_reg.data[i]); variance += v * v; } } variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); if (has_data) { int idx = tid; // because loop ran once at idx=tid VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val_reg.data[i]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[i]); result.data[i] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx] = result; } ``` But this `has_data` and `idx = tid` logic works only if `BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size`. For the generic case where a thread may process multiple vectors, it's wrong. We could make the kernel generic but with explicit handling: if `BLOCK_SIZE >= vec_hidden_size`, use the register path. Actually, we can do something simple: inside the kernel, we can store the loaded vectors in a small array on registers if the number of iterations is small. But the compiler doesn't know the loop bound at compile time. Alternatively, we can use shared memory for the loaded vectors, as discussed earlier. That guarantees single read from global. Let's see if we can implement shmem easily without too much complexity. Shared memory approach: ```cpp template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_vec_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); variance += v * v; } } variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance); __shared__ float s_variance; if (tid == 0) { s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { VecT val = shmem[idx]; VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < VEC_SIZE; ++i) { float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[i]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[i]); result.data[i] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx] = result; } int tail_start = vec_hidden_size * VEC_SIZE; } } ``` In the launcher, we need to pass shared memory size: `vec_hidden_size * sizeof(VecT)`. ```cpp size_t shmem_size = vec_hidden_size * sizeof(Vec<at::Half, VEC_SIZE>); rms_norm_vec_kernel<<<grid, block, shmem_size, stream>>>(...); reasoning
The kernel passes correctness. Let me get the GPU specs first to make sure my optimization aligns well with H100's hardware, and then consider if there are further improvements I can make. The current kernel: 1. Uses warp-shuffle for reduction instead of cub::BlockReduce - this should be faster 2. Does fp16 loads with 128-bit vectors (VEC_SIZE=8 for fp16) 3. Has block_size dynamically calculated based on hidden_size / vec_size, capped at 512 For H100 with 4096 hidden size and 512 tokens: - hidden_size / VEC_SIZE = 4096 / 8 = 512 elements per row in vec units - block_size = min(512, 512) = 512 - Each block handles 1 row with 512 threads - With 512 tokens, we have 512 blocks On H100, we have 132 SMs (for H100 SXM5). Each block has 512 threads = 16 warps. Occupancy-wise, with 64K registers per SM and 255 max registers per thread: - 512 threads per block * 64 registers = 32K registers per block - So we can have 2 blocks per SM = 32 warps per SM out of 64 max - 512 blocks / 132 SMs ≈ 3.88 blocks per SM But actually for memory bandwidth bound kernels, higher occupancy is better. Let me think about using persistent kernels or larger blocks with multiple rows per block. Actually, let me check if I should try reducing block size to 256 to improve occupancy. With 256 threads per block: - 512 / 256 = 2 rows per block - Each block handles 2 tokens - 256 blocks total - 256 threads / 32 warps per block, fewer registers used, potentially more blocks per SM - More parallelism for memory latency hiding Actually, let me consider a different approach. What if I use a multi-row per block strategy? This would: 1. Increase arithmetic intensity by computing multiple reductions per block 2. Allow better memory coalescing if we process multiple rows 3. Reduce synchronization overhead Let me try implementing a version where each block processes multiple rows of the input. This would allow us to: - Use thread blocks more efficiently - Potentially interleave memory loads from different rows to hide latency - Do more work per block while still benefiting from parallelism But before that, let me also check if multi-row per block is worthwhile. For H100 with 3.35 TB/s bandwidth: - 512 * 4096 * 2 bytes (input) + 512 * 4096 * 2 bytes (output) + 4096 * 2 bytes (weight) ≈ 8.4 MB per forward pass - At 3.35 TB/s, theoretical minimum time = 8.4 MB / 3350 GB/s ≈ 2.5 microseconds This is very memory bandwidth bound. So the key is to saturate memory bandwidth. For a simple memory bandwidth kernel, the main optimizations are: 1. Use 128-bit loads - done 2. Minimize overhead from kernel launch and thread/block management 3. Maximize occupancy to hide memory latency On H100, using 256 threads per block might give better occupancy. OK here's the kernel code: ```cpp template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_cached_kernel(...) { const int row = blockIdx.x; const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE; using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>; // Pointers const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size); const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight); VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size); __shared__ float reduce_smem[BLOCK_SIZE / 32]; float variance = 0.0f; // Number of vectors per thread const int vecs_per_thread = (vec_hidden_size + BLOCK_SIZE - 1) / BLOCK_SIZE; // Local cache for input vectors VecT local_vals[vecs_per_thread]; // Load and compute variance #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < vecs_per_thread; ++i) { int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx < vec_hidden_size) { local_vals[i] = input_vec[idx]; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]); variance += v * v; } } } // Reduce variance variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance, reduce_smem); if (threadIdx.x == 0) { reduce_smem[0] = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); float s_variance = reduce_smem[0]; // Compute output using cached values #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < vecs_per_thread; ++i) { int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx < vec_hidden_size) { VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[j]); result.data[j] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx] = result; } } // Tail... } ``` For hidden_size=4096, BLOCK_SIZE=256, VEC_SIZE=8: - vec_hidden_size = 512 - vecs_per_thread = (512 + 255) / 256 = 2... wait, (512 + 255) / 256 = 767 / 256 = 2 (integer division) Hmm, but 512 / 256 = 2 exactly. So vecs_per_thread = 2 when using ceiling division if I'm not careful. Actually: - (512 + 256 - 1) / 256 = 767 / 256 = 2. Good. - For tid = 0: idx = 0, 256. Both valid. - For tid = 255: idx = 255, 511. Both valid. - Perfect. But wait, the ceiling division works when vec_hidden_size / BLOCK_SIZE is not an integer. For the case where it IS an integer (like 512/256=2), we still get 2. Good. Wait, but if vec_hidden_size = 256 and BLOCK_SIZE = 256: - (256 + 255) / 256 = 511 / 256 = 1. Good. - Each thread handles 1 vector. And if vec_hidden_size = 257 and BLOCK_SIZE = 256: - (257 + 255) / 256 = 512 / 256 = 2. - tid 0: idx = 0, 256. Both valid. - tid 1..255: idx = tid (valid), tid+256 (valid only if tid < 257, i.e., tid = 1). - So for most threads, only idx = tid is valid. We have an extra check. The `if (idx < vec_hidden_size)` guard handles this correctly. But the `local_vals` array would be size 2, with most threads only using the first element. This is fine but uses slightly more registers. For the benchmark case where everything divides evenly, it's optimal. One concern: asking the compiler to allocate a variable-length array like `VecT local_vals[vecs_per_thread]` where `vecs_per_thread` is a const int computed at compile time... actually, the compiler should evaluate this at compile time since all values are template parameters. Let me verify: BLOCK_SIZE is a template parameter. VEC_SIZE is a template parameter. hidden_size is a runtime parameter. So `vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE` is runtime. And `vecs_per_thread` is runtime. Oh no, this means `local_vals[vecs_per_thread]` is a variable-length array (VLA), which is not standard C++! And for the benchmark where the loop runs exactly twice and then exits, it should be efficient. Actually, wait. In CUDA, stack arrays (even fixed-size) in device code are stored in local memory, not registers, if their indexing is not compile-time constant! This is a crucial point. If I write `local_vals[i]` where `i` is a runtime variable, the compiler will likely put `local_vals` in local memory (which is backed by global memory, very slow). To keep it in registers, I need compile-time constant indexing. The solution is: 1. Use `#pragma unroll` so the loop is unrolled 2. After unrolling, `i` becomes a compile-time constant in each unrolled iteration 3. Then `local_vals[0]`, `local_vals[1]`, etc. are each distinct variables in registers This should work if the compiler actually unrolls the loop. With `#pragma unroll` and a small constant bound (like 4), it should. But there's another complication: the `if (idx < vec_hidden_size)` check. If vec_hidden_size is runtime, the compiler might not optimize away iterations even after unrolling. However, it can still keep `local_vals` in registers since each unrolled body has a fixed index. Actually, for the common case where the loop body executes MAX_VECS_PER_THREAD times (because hidden_size is large), the compiler will generate code for all iterations. The `if` will be evaluated at runtime, but `local_vals[i]` for each unrolled `i` will be in registers. For the benchmark case where `num_vecs = 2`: - The loop unrolls to 4 iterations - Iteration 0: idx = tid (always < 512), loads local_vals[0], computes variance - Iteration 1: idx = tid + 256 (always < 512), loads local_vals[1], computes variance - Iteration 2: idx = tid + 512 (always >= 512 since tid < 256), skips - Iteration 3: idx = tid + 768 (always >= 512), skips Wait, if BLOCK_SIZE=256 and vec_hidden_size=512: - i=0: idx = tid (0-255), always < 512. ✓ - i=1: idx = tid + 256 (256-511), always < 512. ✓ - i=2: idx = tid + 512 (512-767), always >= 512. ✗ - i=3: idx = tid + 768 (768-1023), always >= 512. ✗ So iterations 2 and 3 are always skipped. Probably not if they're never used. I think this is safe. Let me try it and see if it compiles correctly. Actually, I just realized another issue. If I use a local array and the compiler decides to put it in local memory, performance will tank. Let me check if there's a way to hint register usage. This pattern is very common in CUDA kernels. OK I'm going to write this. Let me also think about whether to keep BLOCK_SIZE=512 or reduce it. For the benchmark with hidden_size=4096: - If BLOCK_SIZE=512, vec_hidden_size=512, vecs_per_thread=1. Each thread handles 1 vector, no loop needed. - If BLOCK_SIZE=256, vec_hidden_size=512, vecs_per_thread=2. Each thread handles 2 vectors. With BLOCK_SIZE=512 and caching: - Each thread caches 1 vector of 8 fp16 = 16 bytes. Easy. - But we can only have 2 blocks per SM due to thread count (512 threads * 2 blocks = 1024 threads per SM... actually max threads per SM on Hopper is 2048, so 4 blocks if registers allow). With 50 registers per thread and 512 threads: 512 * 50 = 25.6K per block. 64K / 25.6K = 2.5. So 2 blocks per SM. 2 blocks * 16 warps = 32 warps per SM out of 64 max. 50% occupancy. With BLOCK_SIZE=256 and caching: - Each thread caches 2 vectors of 8 fp16 = 32 bytes. Easy. - 256 threads * 50 registers = 12.8K per block. 64K / 12.8K = 5 blocks per SM. - But max warps per SM = 64, each block has 8 warps, so max 8 blocks per SM. - Register limited to 5 blocks → 40 warps. 62.5% occupancy. With 512 tokens and BLOCK_SIZE=256: 512 blocks / 132 SMs ≈ 3.88 blocks per SM. This is less than the max of 5, so we can fit all blocks with room to spare. Occupancy is good. With BLOCK_SIZE=512: 512 blocks / 132 SMs ≈ 3.88 blocks per SM. But max blocks per SM is 2 (register limited). So we need 2 passes: one pass runs 264 blocks (132 SMs * 2), then another pass runs the remaining 248 blocks. So BLOCK_SIZE=256 gives better occupancy and might finish faster because all blocks can run concurrently. Wait, but with 3.88 blocks per SM average, can we really fit all 512 blocks at once? The average is just the total divided by SM count. Some SMs would get 4 blocks, some would get 3. With a max of 5 per SM, this is fine. But there's a subtlety: blocks aren't evenly distributed. Some SMs might get more blocks initially. However, as blocks complete, new ones are scheduled. The total throughput is limited by the bottleneck SM. I think BLOCK_SIZE=256 is likely better. Let me use that. Actually wait, another consideration. If I use BLOCK_SIZE=512 and don't cache (read twice), that's 3 memory accesses per row. If I use BLOCK_SIZE=256 and cache (read once), that's 2 memory accesses per row, but with more blocks. The bandwidth savings from caching might outweigh any overhead from the loop. Let me try the caching approach with BLOCK_SIZE=256. One concern: with 2 vectors per thread, do I have enough registers? Each vector is Vec<half, 8> = 16 bytes. 2 vectors = 32 bytes = 16 half registers. Plus temporaries for computation. Total maybe 32-48 registers per thread. Well under the 255 limit. OK let me write the optimized kernel now. Wait, another issue. I should also consider the effect of block size on memory coalescing. With 512 threads, a warp of 32 threads loads 32 contiguous 128-bit vectors = 512 bytes. With 256 threads, a warp loads 32 contiguous vectors = still 512 bytes. The coalescing is the same. With fewer threads per block, each thread does more work, but total warps per block is lower. Since H100 has fast warp scheduling, this might not matter much either way. I think the main win is from register caching. Let me proceed. Let me also consider whether to use `float2` or other special formats. Also, for the reduction, using `__shfl_down_sync` instead of CUB should be faster. I already have that. OK, I'm going to write the final optimized kernel. Here's the full code: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cpp_source = """ #include <torch/extension.h> torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps); """ cuda_source = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cstdint> #include <c10/cuda/CUDAStream.h> template <typename T, int N> struct alignas(sizeof(T) * N) Vec { T data[N]; }; template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = 16; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } return val; } template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val) { int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; static_assert(BLOCK_SIZE <= 1024, "BLOCK_SIZE too large"); __shared__ float shared[BLOCK_SIZE / 32]; val = warp_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(val); if (lane == 0) { shared[warp_id] = val; } __syncthreads(); if (warp_id == 0) { val = (lane < BLOCK_SIZE / 32) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; val = warp_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(val); } if (threadIdx.x == 0) { shared[0] = val; } __syncthreads(); return shared[0]; } // Note: warp_reduce_sum doesn't actually need BLOCK_SIZE template param // but keeping for consistency __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum_val(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int mask = 16; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) { val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask); } return val; } template <int BLOCK_SIZE> __device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum_opt(float val, float* shared) { int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; val = warp_reduce_sum_val(val); if (lane == 0) { shared[warp_id] = val; } __syncthreads(); if (warp_id == 0) { val = (lane < BLOCK_SIZE / 32) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f; val = warp_reduce_sum_val(val); if (lane == 0) { shared[0] = val; } } __syncthreads(); return shared[0]; } // Main kernel with register caching template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_cached_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; if (row >= num_tokens) return; const int tid = threadIdx.x; using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>; const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE; const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size); const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight); VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size); __shared__ float reduce_smem[BLOCK_SIZE / 32]; float variance = 0.0f; // Max vectors per thread: handle up to hidden_size / VEC_SIZE elements // For BLOCK_SIZE=512, max is 2 (for hidden_size up to 8192) // For BLOCK_SIZE=256, max is 4 (for hidden_size up to 8192) #define MAX_VPT 4 VecT local_vals[MAX_VPT]; int valid_count = 0; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < MAX_VPT; ++i) { int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx < vec_hidden_size) { local_vals[i] = input_vec[idx]; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]); variance += v * v; } valid_count = i + 1; } } variance = block_reduce_sum_opt<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance, reduce_smem); if (threadIdx.x == 0) { reduce_smem[0] = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); float s_variance = reduce_smem[0]; // Compute output from cached values #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < MAX_VPT; ++i) { int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx < vec_hidden_size) { VecT w = weight_vec[idx]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[j]); result.data[j] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx] = result; } } #undef MAX_VPT // Tail loop for remaining elements int tail_start = vec_hidden_size * VEC_SIZE; for (int idx = tail_start + tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]); out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } } // Scalar kernel for non-vectorized path template <typename scalar_t, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void rms_norm_scalar_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ out, const scalar_t* __restrict__ input, const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight, float epsilon, int num_tokens, int hidden_size) { const int row = blockIdx.x; if (row >= num_tokens) return; const int tid = threadIdx.x; __shared__ float reduce_smem[BLOCK_SIZE / 32]; float variance = 0.0f; #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); variance += v * v; } variance = block_reduce_sum_opt<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance, reduce_smem); if (threadIdx.x == 0) { reduce_smem[0] = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon); } __syncthreads(); float s_variance = reduce_smem[0]; #pragma unroll 4 for (int idx = tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) { float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]); float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]); out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } } torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps) { TORCH_CHECK(input.is_cuda(), "input must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(weight.is_cuda(), "weight must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(input.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half, "Only fp16 supported"); TORCH_CHECK(weight.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half, "Only fp16 supported"); auto input_2d = input.reshape({-1, input.size(-1)}); int num_tokens = input_2d.size(0); int hidden_size = input_2d.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(input_2d); const int VEC_SIZE = 8; int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE; // Compute block size: aim for each thread to handle ~2 vectors int block_size = (vec_hidden_size + 1) / 2; // ceil(vec_hidden_size / 2) if (block_size > 512) block_size = 512; if (block_size < 128) block_size = 128; // minimum for good occupancy block_size = (block_size + 31) / 32 * 32; // round up to warp dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(block_size); cudaStream_t stream = c10::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); bool use_vec = (hidden_size % VEC_SIZE == 0); bool aligned = false; if (use_vec) { auto inp_ptr = reinterpret_cast<uint64_t>(input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>()); auto wt_ptr = reinterpret_cast<uint64_t>(weight.data_ptr<at::Half>()); aligned = (inp_ptr % (VEC_SIZE * sizeof(at::Half)) == 0) && (wt_ptr % (VEC_SIZE * sizeof(at::Half)) == 0); } // Template dispatch based on block size if (use_vec && aligned) { if (block_size == 512) { rms_norm_cached_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 512><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } else if (block_size == 256) { rms_norm_cached_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 256><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } else if (block_size == 128) { rms_norm_cached_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 128><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } else { rms_norm_cached_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 64><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } } else { if (block_size >= 512) { rms_norm_scalar_kernel<at::Half, 512><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } else if (block_size >= 256) { rms_norm_scalar_kernel<at::Half, 256><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } else { rms_norm_scalar_kernel<at::Half, 128><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>( out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(), eps, num_tokens, hidden_size); } } return out.view_as(input); } """ ``` Wait, there's an issue with my block size calculation. For hidden_size=4096, vec_hidden_size=512. `block_size = (512 + 1) / 2 = 256`. Good. `block_size = 256`. Then rounded to 256. Perfect. For hidden_size=8192, vec_hidden_size=1024. `block_size = (1024 + 1) / 2 = 512`. Good. `block_size = 512`. Rounded to 512. Perfect. For hidden_size=2048, vec_hidden_size=256. `block_size = (256 + 1) / 2 = 128`. Good. `block_size = 128`. Rounded to 128. OK. For hidden_size=1024, vec_hidden_size=128. `block_size = (128 + 1) / 2 = 64`. But then `if (block_size < 128) block_size = 128;` So block_size = 128. Each thread handles 128 elements / 128 threads = 1 vector. That's fine. Actually wait, for hidden_size=1024, with block_size=128, each thread handles 1 vector. MAX_VPT=4 but only 1 is used. The iterations 1,2,3 would be skipped. This is fine. But for the scalar path, block_size minimum of 128 might be too large for very small hidden sizes. A hidden size of 32 with 128 threads means 32 threads do work and 96 threads are idle. Not great, but acceptable for edge cases. Let me also think about the scalar path. The scalar path reads input twice (once for variance, once for output), but there are so few elements that caching in registers would be trivial. In CUDA, macros inside kernels can work, but I need to make sure the compiler optimizes properly. With `#pragma unroll` and compile-time constant loop bounds, `MAX_VPT` should be inlined as 4. Wait, but I'm also doing `#undef MAX_VPT` which is fine. Actually, I just realized an issue: with `#pragma unroll` on the outer loop and `if (idx < vec_hidden_size)` inside, the compiler might not be able to fully optimize away the masked iterations when `vec_hidden_size` is a runtime value. But it should still unroll the loop. The bigger concern: will `local_vals[MAX_VPT]` end up in local memory or registers? In CUDA, arrays declared in device code are typically placed in local memory unless the compiler can prove all accesses use compile-time constant indices and the array is small enough to fit in registers. With `#pragma unroll`, the loop is unrolled, so `local_vals[0]`, `local_vals[1]`, etc. are accessed with compile-time constant indices in each unrolled copy. The compiler should scalarize them into individual registers. However, there's a subtlety: if `valid_count` is used dynamically, or if the compiler can't prove that all accesses are constant-indexed, it might keep the array in local memory. In the unrolled loop: ```cpp local_vals[i] = input_vec[idx]; ... local_vals[i].data[j] ``` After unrolling with i=0,1,2,3: ```cpp local_vals[0] = input_vec[tid]; // iteration 0 ... local_vals[0].data[j] local_vals[1] = input_vec[tid + BLOCK_SIZE]; // iteration 1 ... local_vals[1].data[j] ``` The compiler should see that each `local_vals[k]` is accessed with a constant `k` and scalarize them. This is standard compiler behavior in CUDA. But wait, there's `if (idx < vec_hidden_size)`. If `vec_hidden_size` is runtime, the compiler doesn't know at compile time which iterations are valid. But it can still scalarize the array since the indices are known at unroll time. The `if` just controls whether the values are loaded and computed. Actually, I have some concern about whether the compiler will actually keep `local_vals` in registers across the __syncthreads() barrier. If the compiler thinks the values might be needed by other threads (even though they're local), it could spill to local memory. To be safe, let me use explicit separate variables instead of an array. But then I need to handle the loop unrolling manually. Let me think about how to do this cleanly. One approach: use a switch on the actual vecs_per_thread at runtime. But this only works if we know the maximum. Another approach: just use a single large register to hold all the vectors. For instance: ```cpp // For MAX_VPT=4 and VEC_SIZE=8 // Each thread can hold up to 4 vectors // But I'll just use explicit unrolling with conditional compilation // Actually, for the target benchmark, I know vec_hidden_size / BLOCK_SIZE <= 2 // Let me just write explicit code for up to 2 vectors per thread ``` Hmm, but this gets ugly. Let me try a compromise: use the array approach but add `__ldg` for the loads and see if the compiler handles it. Actually, you know what, I've seen many CUDA kernels use exactly this pattern with local arrays, `#pragma unroll`, and the arrays do end up in registers. The key requirements are: 1. Small array (typically <= 32 bytes total) 2. Constant indexing after loop unrolling 3. No dynamic indexing With MAX_VPT=4 and VecT being 16 bytes, the array is 64 bytes total. This is well within register capacity. The compiler should put it in registers. Let me proceed with the array approach. Actually, I just realized another potential issue. The `#pragma unroll` on the outer loop with `if (idx < vec_hidden_size)` might confuse the compiler's dead-code elimination. The compiler might not know which iterations are always taken, so it generates code for all MAX_VPT iterations, with runtime checks. This could increase instruction count and register pressure. However, for the benchmark case where vec_hidden_size is exactly a multiple of BLOCK_SIZE (or close to it), the compiler might not be able to simplify. Let me consider: with hidden_size=4096, BLOCK_SIZE=256, VEC_SIZE=8: - vec_hidden_size = 512 - For iteration 0: idx = tid, always < 512 when tid < 256. ✓ - For iteration 1: idx = tid + 256, always < 512 when tid < 256. ✓ - For iteration 2: idx = tid + 512, always >= 512. ✗ - For iteration 3: idx = tid + 768, always >= 512. ✗ If the compiler can determine that vec_hidden_size=512 and BLOCK_SIZE=256, it would know iterations 2 and 3 are always skipped. But `vec_hidden_size` is a runtime parameter (passed to the kernel), not a compile-time constant. So the compiler might not optimize this away unless it can trace the values. Actually, in the kernel launch, `hidden_size` is passed as a runtime parameter. The compiler doesn't know it's 4096. So it can't optimize away iterations 2 and 3 at compile time. But here's the thing: even if it generates the checks, the actual overhead is minimal. Iterations 2 and 3 just do a comparison and branch over the load/compute. The registers for `local_vals[2]` and `local_vals[3]` might still be allocated, which increases register pressure. With 4 * 16 bytes = 64 bytes = 32 half registers per thread. Plus other temporaries. Maybe 48-64 registers per thread. With BLOCK_SIZE=256 and 64 registers per thread: 16K per block. 4 blocks per SM. Still good. But if register usage goes up to 80-100 per thread, we might only fit 2 blocks per SM, which is worse. Let me check: with `local_vals[4]` of 16 bytes each = 64 bytes. To be safe, I can write explicit code for the common cases: ```cpp VecT local_val0, local_val1; bool has0 = false, has1 = false; // Iteration 0 int idx0 = tid; if (idx0 < vec_hidden_size) { local_val0 = input_vec[idx0]; has0 = true; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_val0.data[j]); variance += v * v; } } // Iteration 1 int idx1 = tid + BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx1 < vec_hidden_size) { local_val1 = input_vec[idx1]; has1 = true; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_val1.data[j]); variance += v * v; } } // Reduce... // Output iteration 0 if (has0) { VecT w = weight_vec[idx0]; VecT result; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) { float v = static_cast<float>(local_val0.data[j]); float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[j]); result.data[j] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf); } out_vec[idx0] = result; } // Output iteration 1 if (has1) { VecT w = weight_vec[idx1]; ... } ``` But this is extremely verbose and doesn't scale to larger MAX_VPT. The compiler with `-O3` should optimize away the bounds checks when the loop has a fixed trip count (which it does with `#pragma unroll`). Let me verify: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < MAX_VPT; ++i) { int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE; if (idx < vec_hidden_size) { ... } } ``` With MAX_VPT being a template parameter and vec_hidden_size being a runtime value, the compiler might not be able to eliminate the bounds check. But with constant folding, it might. Actually, I can just use `__builtin_assume` if available, or rely on the fact that for our main path, the bounds are always satisfied. For a cleaner approach: let me write a kernel that assumes each thread handles exactly `VPT` vectors, where `VPT` is a compile-time constant. This gives the cleanest code. I'll dispatch based on `vec_hidden_size` divided by block_size: - If vec_hidden_size == block_size: VPT = 1 - If vec_hidden_size == 2 * block_size: VPT = 2 - etc. But I need to handle the general case too. Let me just write the kernel with a template parameter for `VPT` and dispatch to it. Alternatively, I can have the compiler compute VPT via `constexpr`. But blockDim.x is not constexpr. OK let me try yet another design that might be even better. Instead of register caching all input vectors, let me use a simple pipeline: 1. Load vector 0 2. Compute variance for vector 0 3. Load vector 1 4. Compute variance for vector 1 5. ... 6. Reduce 7. Load vector 0 again (or use cached) 8. Compute output for vector 0 But that's the same as register caching. No difference. Let me finalize with the current design. It's already fairly optimized. Let me submit and see the result. Wait, I should run static analysis first. Actually, let me write one more optimized version with these improvements: 1. Use template dispatch for exact VPT (no dynamic bounds checks in the main loop) 2. Add 512-wide block support as the primary path for 4096 hidden size 3. Keep 256-wide block for other sizes 4. Remove unnecessary `if (row >= num_tokens)` if grid is sized correctly Let me write this version.

reasoning
The code passes static check, compile, and correctness tests. Let me think about whether I can further optimize it before submitting. Looking at the current kernel: 1. Uses 128-bit vectorized loads (8x fp16) 2. Has register caching for the VPT kernel variants 3. Uses __launch_bounds__(BLOCK_SIZE, 2) for occupancy 4. Uses warp shuffle based reduction (faster than CUB on H100) One thing I could try: use more threads per block for the 4096 case with VPT=8. Currently with 512 threads and VPT=8, we handle 512*8=4096 vectors, which for hidden=32768 with VEC_SIZE=8 means vec_hidden_size=4096. That's correct. But I'm wondering if using fewer threads with more VPT would actually be better due to register pressure. With VPT=8 and BLOCK_SIZE=512, each thread uses 8 * 16 bytes = 128 bytes of registers just for vector storage. Plus other registers. On H100 with 64K registers per SM and 255 max per thread, 512 threads * ~50-60 registers = 25-30K registers, well within limits. Actually, let me reconsider the best block size for the H100 architecture. H100 has 132 SMs (approx for SXM5). With 512 threads per block, each block needs about 30K registers. An SM can hold ~255 registers/thread * 2048 threads = but that's way more than 64K available. The actual limit is: 64K registers / 512 threads = ~128 registers per thread before running out. So 512-thread blocks are fine. But for latency hiding and occupancy on memory-bound kernels, having more concurrent blocks per SM helps. With 256 threads, we can fit 2 concurrent blocks per SM more easily. Let me check: 64K / 256 = 256 registers/thread max. With ~60 registers per thread, that's only 15K registers, so 4 blocks could run concurrently. For a memory-bound kernel, the key metric is memory bandwidth utilization. More concurrent warps help hide latency. 512 threads = 16 warps. With VPT=8, that's 8 memory loads in a row per thread before the reduction. This should already saturate memory bandwidth well. I think the current implementation is good. Let me also think about using TMA (Tensor Memory Accelerator) on H100, but that's overkill for RMSNorm and would require a lot more code complexity. One more thing: could I use 256-bit loads (16 x fp16 = 256 bits)? That would load a 256-bit vector with a single instruction. But the compiler should already generate LDG.128 instructions for 128-bit loads. For 256-bit, it might use LDG.128 x2 or might issue LDG.E.128.SYS which is slightly different. Given the complexity risk and the fact that 128-bit loads are already good, I'll stick with VEC_SIZE=8. Actually wait, one more optimization opportunity: prefetching weights. In the output loop, we're loading weight vectors from GMEM. These could potentially be prefetched or loaded via LDG while computing. But with VPT, we're already handling VPT weight loads sequentially. Weight should be in L2 cache (broadcast to all blocks), so it's fast. Let me think about another angle: could we fuse the load of the last input vector with the store of the first output vector to maximize memory-level parallelism? Not really since we need all inputs before computing outputs. The kernel is memory bound, and I've already: 1. Eliminated a second GMEM read via register caching (the input is loaded once and reused) 2. Used vectorized loads (8x fp16 = 128 bytes) 3. Optimized block size for high occupancy I think this is near optimal for RMS Norm on H100. Let me submit.

import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline

cpp_source = """
#include <torch/extension.h>
torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps);
"""

cuda_source = r"""
#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <cstdint>
#include <c10/cuda/CUDAStream.h>

template <typename T, int N>
struct alignas(sizeof(T) * N) Vec {
    T data[N];
};

__device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int mask = 16; mask > 0; mask >>= 1) {
        val += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, val, mask);
    }
    return val;
}

template <int BLOCK_SIZE>
__device__ __forceinline__ float block_reduce_sum(float val, float* shared) {
    int lane = threadIdx.x & 31;
    int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5;
    
    val = warp_reduce_sum(val);
    if (lane == 0) {
        shared[warp_id] = val;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    
    if (warp_id == 0) {
        val = (lane < BLOCK_SIZE / 32) ? shared[lane] : 0.0f;
        val = warp_reduce_sum(val);
        if (lane == 0) {
            shared[0] = val;
        }
    }
    __syncthreads();
    return shared[0];
}

template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE, int VPT>
__global__ void __launch_bounds__(BLOCK_SIZE, 2)
rms_norm_vpt_kernel(
    scalar_t* __restrict__ out,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ input,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight,
    float epsilon,
    int hidden_size) {

    const int row = blockIdx.x;
    const int tid = threadIdx.x;
    
    using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>;
    const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE;
    
    const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size);
    const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight);
    VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size);
    
    __shared__ float reduce_smem[BLOCK_SIZE / 32];
    float variance = 0.0f;
    
    VecT local_vals[VPT];
    
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < VPT; ++i) {
        int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE;
        local_vals[i] = input_vec[idx];
        #pragma unroll
        for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) {
            float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]);
            variance += v * v;
        }
    }
    
    variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance, reduce_smem);
    
    float s_variance;
    if (threadIdx.x == 0) {
        s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon);
        reduce_smem[0] = s_variance;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    s_variance = reduce_smem[0];
    
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < VPT; ++i) {
        int idx = tid + i * BLOCK_SIZE;
        VecT w = weight_vec[idx];
        VecT result;
        #pragma unroll
        for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) {
            float v = static_cast<float>(local_vals[i].data[j]);
            float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[j]);
            result.data[j] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf);
        }
        out_vec[idx] = result;
    }
}

template <typename scalar_t, int VEC_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE>
__global__ void __launch_bounds__(BLOCK_SIZE, 2)
rms_norm_generic_kernel(
    scalar_t* __restrict__ out,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ input,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ weight,
    float epsilon,
    int num_tokens,
    int hidden_size) {

    const int row = blockIdx.x;
    if (row >= num_tokens) return;
    const int tid = threadIdx.x;
    
    using VecT = Vec<scalar_t, VEC_SIZE>;
    const int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE;
    
    const VecT* input_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(input + row * hidden_size);
    const VecT* weight_vec = reinterpret_cast<const VecT*>(weight);
    VecT* out_vec = reinterpret_cast<VecT*>(out + row * hidden_size);
    
    __shared__ float reduce_smem[BLOCK_SIZE / 32];
    float variance = 0.0f;
    
    for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) {
        VecT val = input_vec[idx];
        #pragma unroll
        for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) {
            float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[j]);
            variance += v * v;
        }
    }
    
    variance = block_reduce_sum<BLOCK_SIZE>(variance, reduce_smem);
    
    float s_variance;
    if (threadIdx.x == 0) {
        s_variance = rsqrtf(variance / hidden_size + epsilon);
        reduce_smem[0] = s_variance;
    }
    __syncthreads();
    s_variance = reduce_smem[0];
    
    for (int idx = tid; idx < vec_hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) {
        VecT val = input_vec[idx];
        VecT w = weight_vec[idx];
        VecT result;
        #pragma unroll
        for (int j = 0; j < VEC_SIZE; ++j) {
            float v = static_cast<float>(val.data[j]);
            float wf = static_cast<float>(w.data[j]);
            result.data[j] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf);
        }
        out_vec[idx] = result;
    }
    
    int tail_start = vec_hidden_size * VEC_SIZE;
    for (int idx = tail_start + tid; idx < hidden_size; idx += BLOCK_SIZE) {
        float v = static_cast<float>(input[row * hidden_size + idx]);
        float wf = static_cast<float>(weight[idx]);
        out[row * hidden_size + idx] = static_cast<scalar_t>(v * s_variance * wf);
    }
}

torch::Tensor rms_norm_forward(torch::Tensor input, torch::Tensor weight, float eps) {
    TORCH_CHECK(input.is_cuda(), "input must be CUDA");
    TORCH_CHECK(weight.is_cuda(), "weight must be CUDA");
    TORCH_CHECK(input.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half, "Only fp16 supported");
    TORCH_CHECK(weight.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half, "Only fp16 supported");

    auto input_2d = input.reshape({-1, input.size(-1)});
    int num_tokens = input_2d.size(0);
    int hidden_size = input_2d.size(1);
    auto out = torch::empty_like(input_2d);

    const int VEC_SIZE = 8;
    int vec_hidden_size = hidden_size / VEC_SIZE;
    cudaStream_t stream = c10::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream();
    
    bool use_vec = (hidden_size % VEC_SIZE == 0);
    bool aligned = false;
    if (use_vec) {
        auto inp_ptr = reinterpret_cast<uint64_t>(input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>());
        auto wt_ptr = reinterpret_cast<uint64_t>(weight.data_ptr<at::Half>());
        aligned = (inp_ptr % (VEC_SIZE * sizeof(at::Half)) == 0) &&
                  (wt_ptr % (VEC_SIZE * sizeof(at::Half)) == 0);
    }

    if (use_vec && aligned) {
        dim3 grid(num_tokens);
        if (vec_hidden_size == 512) {
            dim3 block(512);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 512, 1><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 256) {
            dim3 block(256);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 256, 1><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 128) {
            dim3 block(128);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 128, 1><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 64) {
            dim3 block(64);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 64, 1><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 1024) {
            dim3 block(512);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 512, 2><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 2048) {
            dim3 block(512);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 512, 4><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else if (vec_hidden_size == 4096) {
            dim3 block(512);
            rms_norm_vpt_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 512, 8><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, hidden_size);
        } else {
            int block_size = 256;
            if (vec_hidden_size < 128) block_size = 128;
            if (vec_hidden_size < 64) block_size = 64;
            dim3 block(block_size);
            rms_norm_generic_kernel<at::Half, VEC_SIZE, 256><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, num_tokens, hidden_size);
        }
    } else {
        int block_size = (hidden_size >= 256) ? 256 : ((hidden_size >= 128) ? 128 : 64);
        dim3 grid(num_tokens);
        dim3 block(block_size);
        if (block_size == 256) {
            rms_norm_generic_kernel<at::Half, 1, 256><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, num_tokens, hidden_size);
        } else if (block_size == 128) {
            rms_norm_generic_kernel<at::Half, 1, 128><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, num_tokens, hidden_size);
        } else {
            rms_norm_generic_kernel<at::Half, 1, 64><<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(
                out.data_ptr<at::Half>(), input_2d.data_ptr<at::Half>(), weight.data_ptr<at::Half>(),
                eps, num_tokens, hidden_size);
        }
    }

    return out.view_as(input);
}
"""

rms_norm_cuda = load_inline(
    name="rms_norm_h100_v4",
    cpp_sources=cpp_source,
    cuda_sources=cuda_source,
    functions=["rms_norm_forward"],
    extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math"],
    verbose=False,
)


class ModelNew(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, hidden_size, eps):
        super().__init__()
        self.eps = eps
        self.weight = nn.Parameter(
            torch.ones(hidden_size, dtype=torch.float16, device="cuda")
        )

    def forward(self, x):
        return rms_norm_cuda.rms_norm_forward(x, self.weight, self.eps)
